The
S^{3}_{b}
partition function of the
FM
theory can be defined recursively as:
26
26

26

Notice that in this work we take all the adjoint fields to be traceless instead of tracefull, differently from the original definition.

\displaystyle Z_{FM}^{(N)}(\vec{X},\vec{Y},\tau,\Delta)=

\displaystyle s_{b}\big(-i\frac{Q}{2}+2\Delta\big)\prod_{j=1}^{N}s_{b}\big(i\frac{Q}{2}-\Delta\pm(Y_{N}-X_{j})\big)s_{b}(-\frac{iQ}{2}+\tau)

\displaystyle s_{b}(-\frac{iQ}{2}+\tau)^{N-1}\prod_{j<k}^{N}s_{b}\big(-i\frac{Q}{2}+\tau\pm(X_{j}-X_{k})\big)\int d\vec{Z}_{N-1}\Delta_{N-1}(\vec{Z})

\displaystyle\prod_{j=1}^{N}\prod_{k=1}^{N-1}s_{b}\big(\frac{iQ}{2}-\frac{\tau}{2}\pm(X_{j}-Z_{k})\big)\prod_{j=1}^{N-1}s_{b}\big(-\frac{iQ}{2}+\frac{\tau}{2}+\Delta\pm(Z_{j}-Y_{N})\big)

\displaystyle Z_{FM}^{(N-1)}\big(\vec{Z},\{Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{N-1}\},\tau,\frac{\tau}{2}+\Delta\big)\,,

(275)

with the basis of the recursion given by:

\displaystyle Z_{FM}^{(1)}(X,Y,\tau,\Delta)=s_{b}\big(-\frac{iQ}{2}+2\Delta\big)s_{b}\big(\frac{iQ}{2}-\Delta\pm(X-Y)\big)\,.

(276)

The vectors
\vec{X}
and
\vec{Y}
are the parameters for the manifest and emergent
U(N)
symmetries respectively, and
\vec{Z}
is the set of parameters for the gauge group
U(N-1)
. The convention for the
3d
partition function is given in appendix
A
.

FM[U(N)]
as
3d
limit of
FE[USp(2N)]

The
FM[U(N)]
theory can be obtained following a
3d
limit reduction of the
FE[USp(2N)]
theory.
We start from the SCI of the
FE[USp(2N)]
theory in (
B.1
) and define the
3d
parameters from the
4d
fugacities as:

\displaystyle x_{j}=e^{2\pi irX_{j}}\quad,\quad y_{j}=e^{2\pi irY_{j}}\quad,\quad z_{j}=e^{2\pi irZ_{j}}

\displaystyle t=e^{2\pi ir\tau}\quad,\quad c=e^{2\pi ir\Delta}\,,

\displaystyle p=e^{-2rb}\quad,\quad q=e^{2rb^{-1}}\,,

(277)

then we perform the limit
r\to 0
and obtain the following relation:

\displaystyle\lim_{r\to 0}I_{FE}^{(N)}(\vec{x},\vec{y},t,c)=C_{N}Z_{FE^{3d}}^{(N)}(\vec{X},\vec{Y},\tau,\Delta)\,,

(278)

where
C_{N}
is a prefactor, which is divergent in the limit
r\to 0
, given as:

\displaystyle C_{N}=\exp\left[\frac{i\pi}{12r}(4\Delta+(1+2N)(-iQ+2(N-1)\tau))\right]\,.

(279)

The
FE^{3d}
theory is given by the same quiver as in
19
where now lines are
3d

{\cal N}=2
chiral multiplets and we also introduce linearly in the superpotential the
USp
monopole of each gauge group. We then shift the parameters
\vec{X},\vec{Y},\vec{Z}
by
(+s)
and perform a real mass deformation sending
s\to+\infty
. This has the effect of Higgsing the gauge symmetries from
USp(2N)
to
U(N)
, landing finally on the
FM[U(N)]
theory:

\displaystyle\lim_{s\to+\infty}Z_{FE^{3d}}^{(N)}(\vec{X},\vec{Y},\tau,\Delta)=K_{N}e^{i\pi(iQ-2\Delta+(N-1)\tau)\sum_{j=1}^{N}(X_{j}+Y_{j})}Z_{FM}(\vec{X},\vec{Y},\tau,\Delta)\,,

(280)

where
K_{N}
is a divergent prefactor:

\displaystyle K_{N}=\exp\big[2isN\pi(iQ-2\Delta+(N-1)\tau)\big]\,.

(281)

Fusion to identity

Using the
3d
limit procedure one can reduce all the identities and properties of the
FE[USp(2N)]
theory into similar properties for the
FM[U(N)]
theory. For example performing such limit on the identity wall relation in (
B.1
) we obtain the following partition function identity:

\displaystyle\int d\vec{Z}_{N}\Delta_{N}(\vec{Z},\tau)Z_{FM}^{(N)}(\vec{X},\vec{Z},\tau,\Delta)Z_{FM}^{(N)}(\vec{Z},\vec{Y},\tau,-\Delta)={}_{\vec{X}}\mathbb{I}_{\vec{Y}}(\tau)\,,

(282)

where the identity operator is defined as:

\displaystyle{}_{\vec{X}}\mathbb{I}_{\vec{Y}}(\tau)=\frac{1}{\Delta_{N}(\vec{X},\tau)}\sum_{\sigma\in S_{N}}\prod_{j=1}^{N}\delta(X_{j}-Y_{\sigma(j)})\,.

(283)

Which consist in two
FM[U(N)]
theories glued together with the addition of a monopole superpotential
{\cal W}=\mathfrak{M}^{+}+\mathfrak{M}^{-}
being dual to an identity operator. We depict this relation as:

\!N\!
\!\!\!N\!\!\!
\!N\!
\Longleftrightarrow
\Pi_{L}
\Pi_{R}
\mathbb{I}
-wall
a
{\cal W}={\cal W}_{\text{gluing}}+\mathfrak{M}^{+}+\mathfrak{M}^{-}

\Pi_{L}

\Delta

\Pi_{R}

-\Delta

a

\tau

The superpotential
{\cal W}_{\text{gluing}}
contains the coupling between the adjoint
a
and the two adjoint operators
\mathsf{A}_{L}
and
\mathsf{A}_{R}
of the left and right
FM[U(N)]
theories.

Mirror self-duality

We can also reduce the mirror self-duality of the
FE[USp(2N)]
theory in (
242
) to obtain a mirror sel-duality for the
FM[U(N)]
theory which is:

\displaystyle Z_{FM}^{(N)}(\vec{X},\vec{Y},\tau,\Delta)=Z_{FM}^{(N)}(\vec{Y},\vec{X},\tau,\Delta)\,.

(288)

Where the two non-abelian global symmetries are swapped, meaning that we have exchanged the manifest and emergent
U(N)
symmetries in the UV representation
20
.

Interesting deformations

Along the lines traced for the
FE[USp(2N)]
theory, we present two types of deformation also for the
FM[U(N)]
theory.
The first deformation is realized by adding the linear superpotential term
\delta{\cal W}=\mathsf{B}_{1,1}
, which has the effect of breaking completely
U(1)_{\Delta}
while it preserves
U(1)_{\tau}
. This consist in the specialization
\Delta=0
which reduces the
FM[U(N)]
theory to the
\mathbb{I}
-wall theory:

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}_{FM}^{(N)}(\vec{x},\vec{y},\tau,\Delta=0)={}_{\vec{X}}\mathbb{I}_{\vec{Y}}(\tau)\,,

(289)

Another interesting case is the specialization obtained by the linear superpotential
\delta{\cal W}=\mathsf{B}_{1,2}
. This has the effect of breaking
U(1)_{\Delta}\times U(1)_{\tau}
down to a
U(1)
subgroup defined by the constraint
\Delta=1-\tau/2
. The
FM[U(N)]
theory is deformed into a bifundamental hyper multiplet coupled to adjoint singlets:

\!N\!
\!N\!
\Pi
{\cal W}=\mathsf{B}_{1,2}
\Longleftrightarrow
\!N\!
\!N\!
b
\color[rgb]{0,0,1}a
\color[rgb]{1,0,0}a
{\cal W}=b({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}a}+{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}a})\tilde{b}+
+Flip[b\tilde{b}]

\Pi

\tau/2

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}a}

2-\tau

{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}a}

2-\tau

b

\tau/2

As a partition function identity this translate into:

\displaystyle Z_{FM}^{(N)}\big(\vec{X},\vec{Y},\tau,\Delta=\frac{\tau}{2}\big)=

\displaystyle s_{b}(-\frac{iQ}{2}+\tau)^{2N-1}\prod_{j<k=1}^{N}\big[s_{b}\big(-\frac{iQ}{2}+\tau\pm(X_{j}-X_{k})\big)

\displaystyle s_{b}\big(-\frac{iQ}{2}+\tau\pm(Y_{j}-Y_{k})\big)\big]s_{b}\big(\frac{iQ}{2}-\frac{\tau}{2}\pm(X_{j}-Y_{k})\big)\,.

(295)

One can also iron a
FM
theory into a bifundamental hypermultiplet also using the deformation
\delta{\cal W}=\mathsf{B}_{2,1}
. This has the effect of breaking
U(1)_{\Delta}\times U(1)_{\tau}
down to a
U(1)
subgroup defined by the constraint
\Delta=\frac{iQ-\tau}{2}
. We have the following property:

\displaystyle Z_{FM}^{(N)}\big(\vec{X},\vec{Y},\tau,\Delta=\frac{iQ-\tau}{2}\big)=s_{b}\big(\frac{iQ}{2}-\tau\big)\prod_{j,k=1}^{N}s_{b}\big(\tau\pm(X_{j}-Y_{k})\big)\,.

(296)

Graphically we have:

The second category of deformations is given by

. Suppose that

\displaystyle X_{i}=\frac{N-M+1-2j}{2}\tau+V\qquad\text{for}\quad i=1,\ldots,N-M\,,

\displaystyle X_{i}=Y_{i-N+M}\qquad\text{for}\quad i=N-M+1,\ldots,N\,.

(300)

We depict the resulting theory as an “asymmetric” bifundamental:

As a identity between partition function we have:

\displaystyle Z_{FM}^{(N)}\big(\vec{X},\big\{\frac{N-1}{2}\tau+V,\ldots,\frac{1-N}{2}\tau+V\big\},\tau,\Delta\big)=

\displaystyle=\prod_{j=2}^{N}s_{b}\big(-\frac{iQ}{2}+j\tau\big)\prod_{j=1}^{N}\big[s_{b}\big(\frac{iQ}{2}-\frac{1-N}{2}\tau-\Delta\pm(X_{j}-V)\big)s_{b}\big(-\frac{iQ}{2}+(j-N)\tau+2\Delta\big)\big]\,.

B.3
3d

{\cal S}

FT[U(N)]

The
FT[U(N)]
theory is a
3d

{\cal N}=4
SCFT denoted by the following symbol:

\!N\!
\!N\!

(307)

The
FT[U(N)]
theory has the following quiver description:

​​
N
-
1
​​​
\!N\!
b_{1}
\cdots
b_{\text{$N$-$1$}}
a_{1}
a_{2}
a_{\text{$N$-$1$}}
a_{N}
{\cal W}=\sum_{i=1}^{N-1}b_{i}(a_{i}+a_{i+1})\tilde{b}_{i}

b_{i},\tilde{b}_{i}

\tau/2

a_{i}

2-\tau

Notice that in the picture above all the adjoint chirals
a_{j}
, for
j=1,\ldots,N-1
, are traceful, while
a_{N}
is traceless.

The UV global symmetry is
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}SU(N)}\times{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}U(1)}^{N-1}\times U(1)_{\tau}
which enhances in the IR to
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}SU(N)}\times{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}SU(N)}\times U(1)_{\tau}
. However we will work with an “off-shell” parameterization so that the manifest symmetry is actually
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}U(N)}\times{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}U(N)}
, this will be useful since we want to perform
U(N)
gaugings of these symmetries. Also we work in the
{\cal N}=2^{\star}
language, where
U(1)_{\tau}
is the
antidiagonal combination of the
U(1)_{C}\times U(1)_{H}
subgroup of the
{\cal N}=4
non-abelian R-symmetry.

The IR spectrum of the theory is given by the two moment maps
\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathsf{A}
and
\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{A}
, that are adjoint for the two
U(N)
global symmetries and carry R-charge
2-\tau
.

Asymmetric
{\cal S}
-wall

Starting from the
FT[U(N)]
theory is possible to perform a deformation with the effect of breaking the two
U(N)
global symmetries. To do this we give a VEV to the moment maps in form of Jordan-block matrices. This VEVs are uniquely specified by two partitions
(\rho,\sigma)
of
N
. In this work we are interested only in the case where one of the two partitions is trivial and the other is such that the
U(N)
symmetry is broken down to
U(M)\times U(1)
, with
M<N
. Let us consider
\vec{X}
to be the set of mass parameters for the unbroken
U(N)
group and
\vec{Y}
those of
U(M)
and
v
for
U(1)
, then this deformation is implemented at the level of the partition function by the specialization:

\displaystyle X_{j}=\frac{N-M+1-2j}{2}\tau+v\quad\text{for}\quad j=1,\ldots,N-M\,,

\displaystyle X_{j}=Y_{j-N+M}\quad\text{for}\quad j=N-M+1,\ldots,N\,.

(311)

The resulting theory is depicted as an asymmetric
FT[U(N)]
theory as:

\!N\!
\!M\!

(312)

Fusion to identity

Gluing together two
FT[U(N)]
theories with an extra adjoint chiral that comes couples to the moment maps charged under the gauge group gives an
\mathbb{I}
-wall as:

\displaystyle\int d\vec{Z}_{N}\Delta_{N}(\vec{Z},\tau)Z_{FT}^{(N)}(\vec{X},\vec{Z},\tau)Z_{FT}^{(N)}(\vec{Z},\pm\vec{Y},\tau)={}_{\vec{X}}\mathbb{I}_{\mp\vec{Y}}(\tau)\,.

(313)

Graphically we can write this identity as:

\!N\!
\!\!\!N\!\!\!
\!N\!
\Longleftrightarrow
-
\pm
\mathbb{I}
-wall
{\cal W}={\cal W}_{\text{gluing}}

(314)

Where the adjoint chiral, with R-charge
\tau
, is coupled to the gauge charged moment maps of the two
FT[U(N)]
theories respectively.

We can also construct asymmetric
\mathbb{I}
-walls by breaking one of the two global
U(N)
symmetries as in
312
. The result is the following:

\!N\!
\!\!\!N\!\!\!
\!M\!
\Longleftrightarrow
-
\pm
\mathbb{I}
-wall
{\cal W}={\cal W}_{\text{gluing}}

(315)

Again, the adjoint chiral is coupled to the moment maps of the two
FT
theories. This identity corresponds to the partition function identity (
313
) with the specialization:

\displaystyle Y_{j}=\frac{N-M+1-2j}{2}\tau+V\quad\text{for}j=1,\ldots,N-M\,,

\displaystyle Y_{j}=W_{j-N+M}\quad\text{for}j=N-M+1,\ldots,N\,,

(316)

where
\vec{W}
is the set of parameters of the
U(M)
global symmetry, while
V
is the parameter of the
U(1)
symmetry.

Appendix C

This appendix is a collection of the

3d

Appendix E
Monopole R-charge

In this section we discuss the monopoles in the SQCD mirror.

Let’s first focus on monopoles with unit magnetic flux
\mathfrak{M}^{\pm 1,0,\cdots,0}
, charged under the
U(1)_{X_{2}-X_{1}}
topological symmetry. In this case we can easily calculate its R-charge by considering the Lagrangian description of the improved bifundamental assuming that we are gauging its manifest symmetry.
By doing so we find:

\displaystyle R[\mathfrak{M}^{(\pm 1,0,\cdots,0)}]

\displaystyle=(N-1)(1-\tau/2)+(1-B_{2})+(N-1)(1-2+\tau)+

\displaystyle+(1-B_{1}-(N-1)\tau/2)+(N-1)(1-\tau)-(N-1)=2-B_{1}-B_{2}\,,

(413)

in the first line we have the improved bifundamental contribution given by the contribution

(N-1)
flavors with R-charge
\tau/2
, one flavor with charge
B_{2}
and one adjoint chiral with charge
2-\tau
.
We then have the contribution of the
V_{1}\tilde{V}_{1}
flavor, the adjoint
a
and the vector multiplet.
As expected this matches the corresponding meson R-charge
R[Q_{1}\tilde{Q}_{2}]=R[Q_{2}\tilde{Q}_{1}]=2-B_{1}-B_{2}
.

We can then consider monopoles with magnetic flux
\mathfrak{M}^{(0,\cdots,\pm 1,0,\cdots,0)}
, charged under the
U(1)_{X_{i}-X_{i-1}}
topological symmetry.
In this case we have to take into account the contribution of the improved bifundamentals on the left and on the right of the node. Thanks to the self-mirror property of the improved bifundamentals we can always calculate this contribution assuming that we are gauging the manifest symmetries of the two improved bifundamentals. So we have:

\displaystyle R[\mathfrak{M}^{(0,\cdots,\pm 1,0,\cdots,0)}]

\displaystyle=(N-1)(1-\tau/2)+(1-B_{i})+(N-1)(1-2+\tau)+

\displaystyle+(N-1)(1-\tau/2)+(1-B_{i+1})+(N-1)(1-2+\tau)+

\displaystyle+(N-1)(1-\tau)-(N-1)=2-B_{i}-B_{i+1}\,,

(414)

in the first two lines we have the contributions of the left and right improved bifundamentals
in the last line, the contribution of the gluing adjoing
a
and of the vector.

As expected this matches the corresponding meson R-charge
R[Q_{i}\tilde{Q}_{i+1}]=R[Q_{i+1}\tilde{Q}_{i}]=2-B_{i}-B_{i+1}
.

To calculate the R-charge of the other monopoles with magnetic flux given by strings of consecutive
\pm 1
we need the contribution of the genearalised bifundamental when we simultaneously gauge its manifest and emergent symmetry which we can’t directly calculate
from the Lagrangian.

For example, the R-charge of the monopole charged under the second and third gauge node is given by:

\displaystyle R[\mathfrak{M}^{(0,\pm 1,\pm 1,0,\cdots,0)}]

\displaystyle=(N-1)(1-\tau/2)+(1-B_{1})+(N-1)(1-2+\tau)+

\displaystyle+(N-1)(1-\tau/2)+(1-B_{4})+(N-1)(1-2+\tau)+

\displaystyle+2(N-1)(1-\tau)-2(N-1)+GB[\pm 1,\pm 1]=

\displaystyle=2-B_{2}-B_{4}\,,

(415)

in the first two lines we have the contribution of the first and fourth genearlised bifundamental which we can calculate using the
Lagrangian description. In the third line we have the contribution of the adjoints and vector mutiplets at the gauged nodes
and the contribution to the third improved bifundamental which we conjecture to be:

\displaystyle GB[\pm 1,\pm 1]=(N-1)(-\tau)\,,

(416)

as the contribution of an ordinary bifundamental flavor of charge
1-\tau/2
.
Assuming (
416
) the charged of the monoples match those of the electric
Q_{i}\tilde{Q}_{i+k}
mesons, we have:

\displaystyle R[\mathfrak{M}^{(0,\ldots,0,\pm 1,\ldots,\pm 1,0,\ldots,0)}]

\displaystyle=(N-1)(1-\tau/2)+(1-B_{1})+(N-1)(1-2+\tau)+

\displaystyle+(N-1)(1-\tau/2)+(1-B_{4})+(N-1)(1-2+\tau)+

\displaystyle+(k+1)(N-1)(1-\tau)-(k+1)(N-1)+kGB[\pm 1,\pm 1]

\displaystyle=2-B_{j}-B_{j+k+1}\,.

(417)

We checked this assumption with the index where we can see that it gives the correct R-charge of monopoles
visibile in the expansion at low
N_{c}
and
N_{f}
,
In particular in the abelian case the R-charge of the monopoles can be computed exactly since the improved bifundamental reduces to just a standard one and we can verify that the assumption is correct in this case.

One can also play a similar game in the
SU(N)
SQCD mirror (see section
6.1
) to establish a map for the baryons. In this case the problem is more complicated and we do not have a complete closed formula for the contribution of an improved bifundamental to the R-charge of a monopole. However we observed empirically that the baryon map can be established by assuming the following formulae:

\displaystyle GB[\pm m,\pm(m-1)]=\begin{cases}m(N-m)\tau+(1-\Delta)\qquad\text{for}\quad 1<m<N\\
(N-1)\tau+(1-\Delta)\qquad\text{for}\quad m=1,N\\
\end{cases}\,,

(418)

\displaystyle GB[\pm m,\pm m]=m(N-m)\tau\,.

(419)

The subcase
m=1
of these assumptions indeed coincide with the result found for the map of the mesons. A more generic formula could be provided by the understanding of the operator map for the dressed baryons in the
SU(N)
SQCD, however we do not have a clear solution to this problem and we address this to a future work.
